\lim_{n}\theta_{n}=+\infty
. By Taylor expansion, we have

\mathbf{L}(V_{n+1})-\mathbf{L}(V_{n})=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}(U^{a}+V_{n})(\delta V_{n})+e_{n}^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}^{\prime}(U^{a}+S_{\theta_{n}}V_{n})(\delta V_{n})+e_{n}^{\prime}+e_{n}^{\prime\prime},

where
e_{n}^{\prime}
is the quadratic error produced by the expansion, and
e_{n}^{\prime\prime}
is the substitution error produced by replacing the basic state
U^{a}+V_{n}
by the smooth one
U^{a}+S_{\theta_{n}}V_{n}
.

When proving the convergence of
\sum_{n}\delta V_{n}
in a certain Sobolev space (here we assume it is
H_{*}^{s}(\Omega)
), we need to start with the following induction hypothesis

Induction hypothesis
(H_{n-1}):\forall 0\leq i\leq n-1,~\|\delta V_{i}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\varepsilon\theta_{i}^{s-\alpha-1}\Delta_{i}
with
\varepsilon\ll 1
and
\Delta_{i}:=\theta_{i+1}-\theta_{i}
,

and then prove a similar estimate for
\delta V_{n}
, that is,
\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\varepsilon\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-1}\Delta_{n}
. In
[
64
]
, this induction step was proven in Lemma 4.14.

To prove the existence in
C^{\infty}
, we need to get an improved estimate in the induction, that is, we start with the induction hypothesis
(H_{n-1})
above, then we need to prove the following “improved estimate” which is better than
(H_{n})

“Improved estimate”:
\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha^{\prime}-1}\Delta_{n}
for some
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha+\gamma
with
\gamma>0
a fixed positive number.

Once this is done, then we can replace the induction hypothesis
(H_{n-1})
by the improved one and repeat this again and again, and finally we will see
\sum_{n}\delta V_{n}
converges in
C([0,T];H_{*}^{s+k\gamma})
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
where
T>0
is independent of
k
. See also the “additional regularity” argument summarized in
[
3
, pp.152]
,
[
54
, Section 3.7]
and adapted by Lindblad
[
38
, (18.40)-(18.43)]
.

First, we find that, if there are only the first two error terms
e_{n}^{\prime},e_{n}^{\prime\prime}
in
[
64
, (4.26)-(4.27)]
, then one can get the following estimates for
\delta V_{n}
based on
(H_{n-1})
above:

\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\Delta_{n}\left(\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-1}\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha,*}+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{n}^{\zeta_{2}(s)-1}\right)+\Delta_{n}\left(\theta_{n}^{5-\alpha}\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha,*}+\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{n}^{\zeta_{2}(6)-1}\right)\varepsilon\theta_{n}^{(s+4-\alpha)_{+}}

where
\zeta_{2}(s):=\max\{(s+2-\alpha)_{+}+12-2\alpha,s+8-2\alpha\}
,
\alpha\geq 12,~\tilde{\alpha}:=\alpha+3,~6\leq s\leq\tilde{\alpha}-2
. So the above estimates give us

\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim(\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha,*}+\varepsilon^{2})\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-1}\Delta_{n}\Rightarrow\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\varepsilon\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-1}\Delta_{n}

(9.1)

for
\varepsilon>0
and
\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha,*}/\varepsilon
sufficiently small. But if we compare the powers of
\theta_{n}
, we find that
\zeta_{2}(6)-1=\max\{3-\alpha,14-2\alpha\}\leq(5-\alpha)-2
and
\zeta_{2}(s)-1\leq(s-\alpha-1)-2
. Based on this and the smoothness of
f
, we can get the improved estimates

\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim(\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha+2,*}+\varepsilon^{2})\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-1-2}\Delta_{n}\Rightarrow\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-1-2}\Delta_{n}

(9.2)

for
\varepsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Then, starting from this new estimate, that is, replacing
\alpha
by
\alpha^{\prime}=\alpha+2
, we can get improved estimates for the errors
e_{n}^{\prime},~e_{n}^{\prime\prime}
and then the total error
E_{n}:=e_{0}+\cdots+e_{n-1}
with
e_{i}:=e_{i}^{\prime}+e_{i}^{\prime\prime}
, the source term
f_{n}
for the linearized problem, and
\delta V_{n}

\displaystyle\|e_{i}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\varepsilon\theta_{i}^{\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)-1}\Delta_{i},~\|S_{\theta_{n}}e_{n-1}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\Delta_{n-1}\varepsilon\theta_{n-1}^{\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)-1}

\displaystyle\|(S_{\theta_{n}}-S_{\theta_{n-1}})E_{n-1}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\Delta_{n-1}\varepsilon\theta_{n-1}^{\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)-1},~\|(S_{\theta_{n}}-S_{\theta_{n-1}})f^{a}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha^{\prime},*}\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha^{\prime}-1}\Delta_{n}

\displaystyle\|f_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\Delta_{n}(\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha^{\prime}-1}\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha^{\prime},*}+\varepsilon\theta_{n}^{\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)-1})

\displaystyle\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\Delta_{n}\left(\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha^{\prime}-1}\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha^{\prime},*}+\varepsilon\theta_{n}^{\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)-1}\right)+\Delta_{n}\left(\theta_{n}^{5-\alpha^{\prime}}\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha^{\prime},*}+\varepsilon\theta_{n}^{\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(6)-1}\right)\theta_{n}^{(s+4-\alpha^{\prime})_{+}},

where
\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)
is defined by replacing
\alpha
with
\alpha^{\prime}
(and hence
\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)\leq\zeta_{2}(s)-4
). Note that
\varepsilon^{2}
in the previous estimates now becomes
\varepsilon
because we use
\|fg\|_{s,*}\lesssim\|f\|_{s,*}\|g\|_{4,*}+\|f\|_{4,*}\|g\|_{s,*}
and the
\varepsilon
arising from
\|f\|_{s,*}
is now replaced by 1. We know that
\zeta_{2}^{\prime}(s)-1\leq(s-\alpha^{\prime}-1)-2
and thus we obtain

\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim(\|f^{a}\|_{\alpha+2+2,*}+\varepsilon)\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha^{\prime}-1-2}\Delta_{n}\Rightarrow\|\delta V_{n}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha^{\prime}-1-2}\Delta_{n}.

(9.3)

Again we replace the induction hypothesis
(H_{n-1})
by
\|\delta V_{i}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha^{\prime\prime}-1}\Delta_{i}
for
i\leq n-1
with
\alpha^{\prime\prime}=\alpha+2\times 2
to proceed the Nash-Moser iteration. Repeat this again and again, we will get
\|\delta V_{i}\|_{s,*}\lesssim\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-2k-1}\Delta_{i}
for
i\leq n
and for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, we can follow the argument in Lindblad
[
38
, (18.40)-(18.43)]
to show that the series
\sum_{n}\delta V_{n}
converges in
C([0,T];H_{*}^{s+2k}(\Omega))
for any
k
with
T
independent of
k
and similarly
\sum_{n}\partial_{t}^{r}\delta V_{n}
converges in
C([0,T];H_{*}^{s-r+2k}(\Omega))
for
0\leq r\leq s
. So the local existence and uniqueness in
C([0,T]\times C^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega}))
is proven, provided that one can construct a smooth data satisfying the compatibility conditions up to infinite order, which will be achieved in Appendix
A.3
. The additional regularity in the time variable can be obtained by differentiating (
1.17
) by
\partial_{t}
repeatedly.

However, there are two extra error terms
e_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime},D_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\delta\Psi_{n}
in
[
64
, (4.26)-(4.27)]
, which are produced because the free surface of
\Omega(t)=\mathbb{T}^{2}\times(-\infty,\varphi(t))
is directly flattened by an explicit diffeomorphism instead of using Lagrangian coordinates.

•

e_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
: modifications in the boundary conditions.
Under the setting of
[
64
]
, the kinematic boundary condition
\partial_{t}\varphi=v\cdot N
and the constraint
H\cdot N=0
are involved in the equation. But the linearization breaks the structure of these two boundary conditions. So after adding the increment in each step of Nash-Moser iteration, extra modifications are required to guarantee these two boundary conditions hold for the basic state
V_{n+1}=V_{n}+\delta V_{n}
.

•

D_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\delta\Psi_{n}
: Dropping the zero-th order term when replacing
\delta V_{n}
by its “good unknown”
\delta\dot{V}_{n}
in the linearized problem.
To solve and prove the tame estimates for the linearized problem for
\delta V_{n}
, the authors of
[
64
]
replaced the variables
\delta V_{n}
by the good unknown (without derivative)
\delta\dot{V}_{n}:=\delta V_{n}-\delta\Psi_{n}(\partial_{3}V_{n}/\partial_{3}\Phi_{n})
and drop a zero-th order term to get the so-called effective linearized problem. This step produces the problematic error
D_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\delta\Psi_{n}
due to that dropped term.
This is a bad term, as it contributes to
\varepsilon^{2}\theta_{n}^{s-\alpha-1}\Delta_{n}
which prevents us improving the estimates of
\delta V_{n}
.

Under the setting of Lagrangian coordinates, these two terms are not needed. For example, there were no such error terms in Lindblad
[
38
,
40
]
where the local existence for smooth solutions to Euler equations are proved by using Nash-Moser iteration.

Indeed, when using Lagrangian coordinates, the material derivative becomes
\partial_{t}
and the kinematic boundary condition then becomes “
\partial_{t}
is a tangential derivative”. In other words, there is no description for the position of
\partial\Omega(t)
in Lagrangian coordinates. Instead, the information of free surface is reflected by
\eta
which is defined as the flow map of velocity. Besides, Lemma
1.1
shows that the magnetic field is completely determined by its initial data and the flow map, i.e.,
b=J^{-1}(b_{0}\cdot\partial)\eta
, and thus the MHD system (
1.17
) only includes the variables
\eta,v,q
. The boundary constraint is now just
b_{0}^{3}=0
that has no dependence on time. Hence, we don’t have to consider the propagation of this condition when doing the iteration. The formulation (
1.17
) does not affect the tame estimates, as we can still get the energy of
J^{-1}(b_{0}\cdot\partial)\delta\eta
together with
\delta v
and
\delta q
as in (2.4). In fact, the linearized momentum equation still has the form parallel to the nonlinear problem
\partial_{t}\delta v-(b_{0}\cdot\partial)^{2}\delta\eta+\nabla_{A}\delta Q=\cdots
(with
\rho,J
omitted) and multiplying this by
\delta v
and integrating by parts in section
2.2
-
2.3
gives energy estimates. When the variation operator
\delta
falls on
J^{-1}
, we can use
\rho_{0}=RJ
to get
\delta\rho_{0}=R\delta J+JR^{\prime}(q)\delta q
and the positivity of density
R
and then
\delta J
can be expressed in terms of
\delta q
. This shows why we can avoid the modification error
e_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

The error
D_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\delta\Psi_{n}
can also be avoided. In Lagrangian coordinates, the analogue of
\delta\dot{V}_{n}
is equal to
\delta V_{n}-\delta\eta_{n}\cdot\nabla_{A}V_{n}
. When doing tame estimates, we may still use
\delta\dot{V}_{n}
to do calculation, but we finally derive the energy inequality for
\delta V_{n}
instead of
\delta\dot{V}_{n}
. The reason is that their difference can be estimated by
\|\delta\eta_{n}\|_{s,*}\|\nabla_{A}V_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\nabla_{A}V_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}}\int_{0}^{T}\|\delta v_{n}\|_{s,*}
. The advantange is that
\delta\eta_{n}
has the same regularity as
\delta v_{n}
so we don’t have derivative loss for this term; while under the setting of
[
64
]
, the regularity for
\delta\Psi_{n}
is not the same as
\partial_{t}\delta\Psi_{n}
.

On the other hand, dropping the zero-th order term
\delta\eta_{n}\cdot\nabla_{A}V_{n}
when solving the linearized problem is not necessary. Indeed, the dropped term is a zero-th order term that can be moved to the right side, and the estimates for the “effective” linearized problem (cf.
[
64
, (3.25)]
) shows that the interior inhomogeneous term comes with an time integral. Thus, one can solve the linearized problem by using contraction mapping theorem in some
[0,T_{0}^{\prime}]
. This
T_{0}^{\prime}
may be smaller than
T_{0}
obtained in
[
64
, Theorem 3.1]
, but since the system is linear, it can be continued to the full time interval
[0,T_{0}]
.

In Lagrangian coordinates, we have
\delta\eta_{n}
is the flow map of
\delta v_{n}
, and one can alternatively use the Galerkin method presented in Gu-Luo-Zhang
[
23
, Section 7.1]
to prove the local existence of the linearized system. If we expand
\delta\eta
to be
\sum_{j}Z_{j}(t)e_{j}(y)
for some Galerkin basis
\{e_{j}\}
, then the zero-th order term dropped in
[
64
]
is just equal to
\sum_{j}Z_{j}(t)e_{j}(y)\cdot(\text{coefficients only involving basic state})
and the velocity becomes
\sum_{j}Z_{j}^{\prime}(t)e_{j}(y)
. The momentum equation is still an ODE involving
Z_{j}^{\prime\prime}(t),~Z_{j}(t)
and terms involving magentic field and pressure. Thus, we no longer need to take into account the error
D_{n+\frac{1}{2}}\delta\Psi_{n}
as in
[
64
]
. Based the above discussion, we claim that Theorem
1.2
holds.

9.2
Continuation of smooth solutions

To prove the continuation criterion for smooth solution stated in Theorem
1.5
, we need to prove the energy inequality in the following form

\mathcal{E}_{m}(t)\leq\mathcal{E}_{m}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}P(\mathcal{E}_{m-1}(t))\mathcal{E}_{m}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t,

(9.4)

that is, the energy inequality for
\mathcal{E}_{m}(t)
is linear in
\mathcal{E}_{m}(t)
(the highest order terms). Once this energy inequality is proven, then Theorem
1.5
follows by using contradiction. Indeed, for a given
m
, if
T_{m}^{*}<+\infty
and we still have
\mathcal{E}_{m-1}(t)\leq M
in
[0,T_{m}^{*}]
for some constant
M>0
, then the above inequality shows that
\mathcal{E}_{m}(t)\leq\mathcal{E}_{m}(0)(1+P(M)te^{P(M)t})
in
[0,T_{m}^{*}]
and thus it still remains bounded, which contradicts with the maximality of
T_{*}^{m}
.

To verify the linearity of the highest order terms in the energy estimates, it suffices to analyze the commutators, either the terms in the modified good unknowns or the reduction of normal derivatives on the boundary, such that the highest order term is linear. For simplicity of notations, we only verify the case
m=8
and
\partial_{*}^{I}=\overline{\partial}{}^{8}
for the commutators appearing in the interior estimates, and
\partial_{*}^{I}=\partial_{3}^{4}
for commutators arising in the reduction of normal derivatives on the boundary.

Commutators
[\overline{\partial}{}^{8},f]\mathfrak{D}g
for
f,g\in H_{*}^{8}(\Omega)
.

Here
\mathfrak{D}
is a tangential derivative, such as
\partial_{t}
or
(b_{0}\cdot\partial)
. Such commutators appear when the energy terms are produced. The terms included in such commutators have the form
\overline{\partial}{}^{N}f\overline{\partial}{}^{8-N}\mathfrak{D}g
for
1\leq N\leq 8
. Indeed, when
1\leq N\leq 4
, we put
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
norm on
f
and
L^{2}(\Omega)
norm on
g
. When
5\leq N\leq 8
, we put
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
norm on
g
and
L^{2}(\Omega)
norm on
f
. By using the definition of anisotropic Sobolev space and the Sobolev embedding
H^{2}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, we will find

\|[\overline{\partial}{}^{8},f]\mathfrak{D}g\|_{0}\lesssim\|f\|_{8,*}\|g\|_{5,*}+\|f\|_{7,*}\|g\|_{6,*}+\|f\|_{6,*}\|g\|_{7,*}+\|f\|_{5,*}\|g\|_{8,*},

which is linear in
\|\cdot\|_{8,*}
norm.

The error term
I_{3}
when producing the energy of
q
.

In (
4.21
) and (
5.37
), we have the following term

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\frac{JR^{\prime}(q)}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{*}^{I}\eta_{p}\hat{A}^{lp}\partial_{l}Q\partial_{*}^{I}\partial_{t}q\,\mathrm{d}y\,\mathrm{d}t,

which is controlled after integrating by parts in
\partial_{t}
. So, it introduces a term without time integral

\int_{\Omega}\frac{JR^{\prime}(q)}{\rho_{0}}\partial_{*}^{I}\eta_{p}\hat{A}^{lp}\partial_{l}Q\partial_{*}^{I}q\,\mathrm{d}y\lesssim\varepsilon\|q\|_{8,*}^{2}+\frac{1}{4\varepsilon}\|\partial_{*}^{I}\eta\|_{0}^{2}\left\|\frac{JR^{\prime}(q)}{\rho_{0}}\hat{A}^{lp}\partial_{l}Q\right\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}.

Note that the terms in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
norm only have at most one derivative, so this term is bounded by
\|\cdot\|_{6,*}
norms of
\eta,\rho_{0},Q
. Next using
\eta=\text{Id}+\int_{0}^{T}v\,\mathrm{d}t
gives the energy estimate that is linear in
\|\cdot\|_{8,*}
norm.

Commutators in modifided Alinhac good unknowns.

This is the most involved part in the paper. We take the case
\partial_{*}^{I}=\overline{\partial}{}^{8}
, i.e., the most difficult case, for an example. Recall the calculations in section
5.1.1
and we find that the control of
C_{3},C_{4},C_{5}
is parallel to
C_{1}
and the control of
C_{6}
is parallel to
C_{2}
, so we only focus one the control of
C_{1}(f)
and
C_{2}(f)
.

Recall that
C_{1}(f)=8\nabla_{A}^{i}(\nabla_{A}^{p}\overline{\partial}{}f)\overline{\partial}{}^{7}\eta_{p}-8([\overline{\partial}{}^{6},A^{lp}A^{mi}]\partial_{m}\eta_{p})\overline{\partial}{}\partial_{l}f
. In the first term, the top-order part has the form
P(\partial\eta)\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}\eta\overline{\partial}{}^{7}\eta
, whose
L^{2}(\Omega)
norm can be controlled by using
H^{1}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow L^{6}(\Omega)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f\overline{\partial}{}^{7}\eta\|_{0}^{2}=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f~\overline{\partial}{}^{7}\eta~\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f~\overline{\partial}{}^{7}\eta\,\mathrm{d}y

(9.5)

\displaystyle\overset{\overline{\partial}{}}{=}

\displaystyle-\int_{\Omega}\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}^{2}f~\overline{\partial}{}^{7}\eta~\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f~\overline{\partial}{}^{6}\eta\,\mathrm{d}y-\int_{\Omega}\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f~\overline{\partial}{}^{8}\eta~\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f~\overline{\partial}{}^{6}\eta\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}^{2}f\|_{1}\|\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f\|_{1}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{7}\eta\|_{0}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{6}\eta\|_{1}+\|\partial^{2}\overline{\partial}{}f\|_{1}^{2}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{8}\eta\|_{0}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{6}\eta\|_{1}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\eta\|_{8,*}\|\eta\|_{7,*}\|f\|_{7,*}\|f\|_{8,*}+\|\eta\|_{8,*}^{2}\|f\|_{7,*}^{2}\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{8}(t)\mathcal{E}_{7}(t).

In the second term of
C_{1}(f)
, we need to control
\|[\overline{\partial}{}^{6},A^{lp}A^{mi}]\partial_{m}\eta_{p}\|_{0}
, whose top-order part reads
P(\partial\eta,\overline{\partial}{}\eta)(\overline{\partial}{}^{N}\partial\eta)(\overline{\partial}{}^{6-N}\partial\eta)
for
1\leq N\leq 6
. So it is controlled by

P(\|\eta\|_{7,*})(\|\overline{\partial}{}^{6}\partial\eta\|_{0}\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\overline{\partial}{}^{5}\partial\eta\|_{0}\|\overline{\partial}{}\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\partial\eta\|_{0}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}})\leq P(\|\eta\|_{7,*})\|\eta\|_{8,*}

The control of
C_{2}(f)
is divided into three parts in section
5.1.1
, and
C_{2}(v)
,
C_{21}(Q)
and
C_{22}(Q)
are already controlled in our desired form. Now we analyze
C_{23}(Q)
that has the form
\overline{\partial}{}^{N}\hat{A}^{lp}\overline{\partial}{}^{7-N}\partial_{l}Q
for
1\leq N\leq 6
. For
N=5,6
, we put
L^{2}(\Omega)
norm on
A
and
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
norm on
Q
; and put
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
norm on
A
and
L^{2}(\Omega)
norm on
Q
for
1\leq N\leq 2
. When
n=3,4
, we need a observation that when
l=3
,
\hat{A}^{3p}=\overline{\partial}{}\eta\times\overline{\partial}{}\eta
only contains tangential derivative; and when
l=1,2
,
\partial_{l}
itself is tangential. So when
N=4
, we have either
(\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\partial\eta\times\overline{\partial}{}\eta)\overline{\partial}{}^{4}Q
or
(\overline{\partial}{}^{5}\eta\times\overline{\partial}{}\eta)\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\partial Q
which is controlled by

\|\overline{\partial}{}^{4}\partial\eta\|_{0}\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{4}Q\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\overline{\partial}{}^{5}\eta\|_{L^{3}}\|\partial\eta\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\partial Q\|_{L^{6}}\leq\|\eta\|_{6,*}^{2}\|Q\|_{8,*}.

Similar approach applies to the modified good unknowns for other derivatives which shoule be easier than the case
\partial_{*}^{I}=\overline{\partial}{}^{8}
. So we show that, for a function
f\in H_{*}^{8}(\Omega)
and its “modified” good unknown
\mathbf{F}
with respect to
\partial_{*}^{I}
, the following property holds

\partial_{*}^{I}(\nabla_{A}^{i}f)=\nabla_{A}^{i}\mathbf{F}+C^{i}(f),\text{ with }\|C(f)\|_{8,*}\leq P(\mathcal{E}_{7}(t))\mathcal{E}_{8}(t),

where
\mathcal{E}_{m}(t)
is the energy functional defined in (
1.21
).

The estimates for the modification terms
\Delta_{f}
on
\Gamma
.

In the boundary estimates in section
5.2.2
, we have to control the
L^{2}(\Gamma)
norms of
\Delta_{Q},\Delta_{v}
and
\partial_{t}(J\Delta_{Q})
. Indeed, the analysis in (
5.47
) and the Sobolev inequality
|f|_{W^{1,\infty}}\leq\|f\|_{5,*}
have already make the energy estimates linear in the top-order term, that is,

|\Delta_{Q}|_{0}+|\Delta_{v}|_{0}+|\partial_{t}(J\Delta_{Q})|_{0}\leq P(\mathcal{E}_{7}(t))\mathcal{E}_{8}(t).

Commutators arising in the reduction of normal derivatives.

When there is a normal derivative
\partial_{3}
included in
\partial_{*}^{I}
, we need to repeatedly use the MHD system to reduce one normal derivative to one tangential derivative, which then produces lots of commutators. We take
\partial_{*}^{I}=\partial_{3}^{4}
for an example. There are two types of commutators in section
4.3
and we need to control their
|\cdot|_{0}
norm.

•

[\partial_{3}^{3},A^{3i}]\partial_{3}f
. This is easy to control, it equals to
\partial_{3}^{3}A^{3i}\partial_{3}f+3\partial_{3}^{2}A^{3i}\partial_{3}^{2}f+3\partial_{3}A^{3i}\partial_{3}^{3}f
and is then controlled by

\displaystyle\|[\partial_{3}^{3},A^{3i}]\partial_{3}f\|\leq

\displaystyle~P(|\overline{\partial}{}\eta,\overline{\partial}{}\partial\eta|_{L^{\infty}})\left(|\partial_{3}^{3}\eta|_{1}|\partial_{3}f|_{L^{\infty}}+|\partial_{3}^{2}\overline{\partial}{}\eta|_{L^{4}}|\partial_{3}^{2}f|_{L^{4}}+|\partial_{3}\overline{\partial}{}\eta|_{L^{\infty}}|\partial_{3}^{3}f|_{0}\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~P(\|\eta\|_{7,*})(\|\eta\|_{4}+\|f\|_{4}),

(9.6)

where we use the fact that
A^{3i}=\overline{\partial}{}\eta\times\overline{\partial}{}\eta.

•

[\partial_{3}^{2},A^{Lp}A^{mi}]\partial_{3}\partial_{m}\eta_{p}
,
L=1,2
. The analysis is similar as above, because the top-order term has the form
(\partial_{3}^{3}\eta)(\partial_{3}^{2}\eta)P(\partial\eta)
.

Summarizing the above analysis, we show that, for
m\geq 8
, the energy inequality is in fact

\mathcal{E}_{m}(T)\leq\mathcal{E}_{m}(0)+P(\mathcal{E}_{m-1}(T))\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{E}_{m}(t)P(\mathcal{E}_{m-1}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t,

so we have proved a continuation criterion.

9.3
Passing to the case of initial data satisfying compatibility conditions up to finite order

Finally, we prove Theorem
1.6
. First we recall that, we say the initial data
(v_{0},b_{0},Q_{0})
satisfies the compatibility conditions up to
k
-th order, if
Q_{j}:=\partial_{t}^{j}Q|_{t=0}=0
on
\Gamma
holds for
0\leq j\leq k
; and we say
(v_{0},b_{0},Q_{0})
satisfies the compatibility conditions up to infinite order if
Q_{j}:=\partial_{t}^{j}Q|_{t=0}=0
on
\Gamma
holds for all
j\geq 0,~j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Given an integer
m\geq 8
and initial data
U_{0}:=(v_{0},b_{0},Q_{0})
satisfying the compatibility conditions up to
(m-1)
-th order, assume we already find a sequence of smooth data
U_{0}^{(n)}:=(v_{0}^{(n)},b_{0}^{(n)},Q_{0}^{(n)})
satisfying the compatibility conditions up to infinite order, such that
\|U_{0}^{(n)}-U_{0}\|_{m}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Now we introduce the following procedure:

1.

For each
n
, using Theorem
1.2
, we know there exists a unique smooth solution
U^{(n)}(t,\cdot)\in C^{\infty}([0,T^{(n)}];\Omega)
for some
T^{(n)}>0
. The lifespan
T^{(n)}
may depend on
n
at this point.

2.

By our a priori estimates (Theorem
1.3
), we have
\|U^{(n)}(t,\cdot)\|_{m,*}\leq P(\|U^{(n)}(0,\cdot)\|_{m,*})\leq P(\|U_{0}^{(n)}\|_{m})
provided the existence and the right side is independent of
n
. Using the continuation criterion (Theorem
1.5
), we can extend the solution a bit more after
T^{(n)}
, until the a priori bounds become invalid. So,
the lifespan of
\{U^{(n)}(t)\}
in
H_{*}^{8}(\Omega)
has a lower bound
T_{0}
independent of
n
.

3.

Using Theorem
1.4
, we know
\|U^{(k)}(t,\cdot)-U^{(l)}(t,\cdot)\|_{m-2,*}\leq C(\|U_{0}^{(k)}-U_{0}^{(l)}\|_{m-2})P(\|U_{0}^{(k)},U_{0}^{(l)}\|_{m})
. Here
C(\cdot)>0
is a continuous function of its arguments and
C(x)\to 0
as
|x|\to 0
. This is because the equations for
U^{(k)}-U^{(l)}
are
linear
in
U^{(k)}-U^{(l)}
, so
(\partial_{t}^{k}U^{(k)}-\partial_{t}^{k}U^{(l)})|_{t=0}
can be expressed linearly in terms of
U_{0}^{(k)}-U_{0}^{(l)}
. When
k,l\to\infty
, the right side converges to zero, and then
\{U^{(n)}(t)\}
has a limit
U(t)\in H_{*}^{m-2}(\Omega)
in
[0,T_{0}]
.

4.

The limit
U(t)
must be a solution with the given initial data
U_{0}\in H^{m}(\Omega)
by using Sobolev embedding. Since each
U^{(n)}(t)
belogns to
H_{*}^{m}(\Omega)
in
[0,T_{0}]
, we know by the a priori bounds, the limit
U(t)
also satisifies
\|U(t)\|_{m,*}\leq P(\|U_{0}\|_{m})
in some
[0,T_{m}]
with
T_{m}
only depending on
\|U_{0}\|_{m}
,
c_{0}
in the Rayleigh-Taylor sign, and
A_{0}
in the equation of state.

Therefore,
under the assumption of Theorem
1.6
, for each given data
U_{0}:=(v_{0},b_{0},Q_{0})
satisfying the compatibility conditions up to
(m-1)
-th order, we prove that there exists a unique solution to (
1.17
) in
C([0,T_{m}],H_{*}^{m}(\Omega))
for some
T_{m}
only depending on
\|U_{0}\|_{m}
,
c_{0}
in the Rayleigh-Taylor sign, and
A_{0}
in the equation of state. This solution also satisfies the a priori bounds, continuous dependence on initial data and uniqueness as stated in Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
.

Appendix A
Construction of smooth data satisfying compatibility conditions up to infinite order

In the appendix, we prove the existence of smooth data satisfying compatibility conditions up to infinite order. Assume
m\geq 8
is an integer and we are given an initial data
(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
satisfying the compatibility conditions up to
(m-1)
-th order in
H^{m}(\Omega)
, where
P_{0}
is the total pressure, while the fluid pressure is denoted by
p_{0}=P_{0}-\frac{1}{2}|b_{0}|^{2}
. For simplicity of notations, we assume
R^{\prime}(q)/R|_{R=1}=1
.

The initial constraints and compatibility conditions for
(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
are

•

(Compatibility conditions)
P_{(j)}:=\partial_{t}^{j}P|_{t=0}=0
on
\Gamma
,
0\leq j\leq m-1
.

•

(Initial constraints)
\text{div }b_{0}=0
in
\Omega
,
b_{0}^{3}|_{\Gamma}=0
, and the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition
-\frac{\partial P_{0}}{\partial N}\geq c_{0}>0
on
\Gamma
.

A.1
Compatibility conditions in terms of initial data

First we express the compatibility conditions in terms of
w_{0},b_{0},P_{0}
. The zero-th order compatibility condition is
P_{0}|_{\Gamma}=0
. To express the first-order compatibility condition, we use
P=p+\frac{1}{2}|b_{0}|^{2}
, where
p
is the fluid pressure, the continuity equation
\partial_{t}p+\text{div }v=0
, and
\partial_{t}b=b\cdot\nabla v-b\text{div }v
to get (we omit the coefficient
A
as it equals to Id at
t=0
. The appearance of
A
does not affect the essence of the proof.)

\text{div }w_{0}=-|b_{0}|^{2}\text{div }w_{0}+(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})w_{0}\cdot b_{0}\text{ on }\Gamma,

(A.1)

and we define the right side of (
A.1
) to be the functional
\mathcal{M}_{-1}(w_{0},b_{0})
.
Next we take divergence in the momentum equation to get a wave equation of
P

\partial_{t}^{2}P-\Delta P=\partial_{t}^{2}(\frac{1}{2}|b|^{2})+\nabla_{i}w^{j}\nabla_{j}w^{i}-\nabla_{i}b^{j}\nabla_{j}b^{i},~~P_{0}|_{\Gamma}=0

(A.2)

and again use
\partial_{t}b=b\cdot\nabla v-b\text{div }v
and
\partial_{t}v\sim(b_{0}\cdot\partial)b-\nabla P
to get

\partial_{t}^{2}P-\Delta P=\mathcal{M}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0})~~\text{ on }\{t=0\},

where
\mathcal{N}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0}):=\nabla_{i}w_{0}^{j}\nabla_{j}w_{0}^{i}-\nabla_{i}b_{0}^{j}\nabla_{j}b_{0}^{i}
and
\mathcal{M}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
is defined by

\mathcal{M}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0}):=|b_{0}|^{2}\Delta P_{0}-(b_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}P_{0}+(b_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}b_{0}\cdot b_{0}+\mathcal{R}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0})

and
\mathcal{R}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0})
only contains the first-order derivative of
b_{0}
and
v_{0}

\mathcal{R}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0}):=P_{0}(b_{0})\left((\nabla^{i_{1}}w_{0})(\nabla^{i_{2}}w_{0})+(\nabla^{j_{1}}b_{0})(\nabla^{j_{2}}b_{0})\right),

where
P_{0}(b_{0})
is a polynomial of
b_{0}
only contains cubic and quadratic terms, and
(i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2})=(1,1,0,0)\text{ or }(0,0,1,1)
.

We see that the 2nd-order compatibility condition
P_{(2)}:=\partial_{t}^{2}P|_{t=0}=0
on
\Gamma
is equivalent to (we use
b_{0}^{3}|_{\Gamma}=0
)

-\Delta P_{0}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})=|b_{0}|^{2}\Delta P_{0}-(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})^{2}P_{0}+(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})^{2}b_{0}\cdot b_{0}+\mathcal{R}(w_{0},b_{0})\text{ on }\Gamma.

(A.3)

Taking time derivatives in the wave equation above repeatedly we get for
k\geq 1

P_{(k+2)}-\Delta P_{(k)}=\mathcal{M}_{k}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{k}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})\text{ in }\Omega,

where, after long and tedious calculations, the functionals
\mathcal{M}_{k},\mathcal{N}_{k}
have the following form for
r\geq 1

\displaystyle k=2r-1,~~\mathcal{M}_{2r-1}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})=

\displaystyle-|b_{0}|^{2}\Delta^{r}\text{div }w_{0}+(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})^{2}\Delta^{r-1}\text{div }w_{0}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=2}^{r+1}\underbrace{b_{0}^{i_{1}}\cdots b_{0}^{i_{2l}}(\nabla^{2r+1}w_{0})}_{<2^{l}\text{ terms}}+\mathcal{R}_{2r-1}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0}),

(A.4)

\displaystyle k=2r,~~\mathcal{M}_{2r}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})=

\displaystyle~|b_{0}|^{2}\Delta^{r+1}P_{0}-(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})^{2}\Delta^{r}P_{0}+\mathcal{R}_{2r}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0}),

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=2}^{r+1}\underbrace{(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})^{r+2}(\nabla^{r}b_{0})b_{0}^{i_{1}}\cdots b_{0}^{i_{2l}}+(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})^{2}(\nabla^{2r}P_{0})b_{0}^{j_{1}}\cdots b_{0}^{j_{2l}}}_{<2^{l}\text{ terms }};

(A.5)

and the term
\mathcal{R}_{k}
, where every top-order term has
(k+1)
-th order derivative, has the following form

\mathcal{R}_{k}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})=P_{k}(b_{0})\left(C^{k}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{m},j_{1}\cdots j_{n},k_{1}\cdots k_{l}}(\nabla^{i_{1}}w_{0})\cdots(\nabla^{i_{m}}w_{0})(\nabla^{j_{1}}b_{0})\cdots(\nabla^{j_{n}}b_{0})(\nabla^{k_{1}}P_{0})\cdots(\nabla^{k_{l}}P_{0})\right),

where
P_{k}(\cdot)
is a polynomial of its arguments and the lowest power is 4 and the indices above satisfy

\displaystyle 1\leq i_{1},\cdots,i_{m},j_{1},\cdots,j_{n}\leq k+1,0\leq k_{1},\cdots,k_{l}\leq k+1,

\displaystyle i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}+j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n}+k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l}=k+1.

The term
\mathcal{N}_{k}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
has the following form

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{k}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})=

\displaystyle~P_{k,1}(b_{0})(\nabla^{1+2\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\rfloor}w_{0})(\nabla w_{0})+P_{k,2}(b_{0})(\nabla^{2\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil}P_{0})(\nabla w_{0})+P_{k,0}(b_{0})(\nabla^{k+1}b_{0})(\nabla w_{0})

\displaystyle+P^{\prime}_{k}(b_{0})D^{k}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{m},j_{1}\cdots j_{n},k_{1}\cdots k_{l}}\left((\nabla^{i_{1}}w_{0})\cdots(\nabla^{i_{m}}w_{0})(\nabla^{j_{1}}b_{0})\cdots(\nabla^{j_{n}}b_{0})(\nabla^{k_{1}}P_{0})\cdots(\nabla^{k_{l}}P_{0})\right),

where
P_{k,1}(\cdot),P_{k,2}(\cdot),P_{k}^{\prime}(\cdot)
are polynomials of their arguments and
P_{k,0}(\cdot)
is a polynomial of its arguments and the lowest power is 2. The indices above satisfy

\displaystyle 1\leq i_{1},\cdots,i_{m},j_{1},\cdots,j_{n}\leq k,0\leq k_{1},\cdots,k_{l}\leq k,

\displaystyle i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}+j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n}+k_{1}+\cdots+k_{l}=k+1.

So the
k
-th compatibility condition can be equivalently written as

\displaystyle k=2r+1,~~\Delta^{r}\text{div }w_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r}\Delta^{j}(\mathcal{M}_{2r-1-2j}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{2r-1-2j}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0}))\text{ on }\Gamma,

(A.6)

\displaystyle k=2r,~~-\Delta^{r}P_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{r-1}\Delta^{j}(\mathcal{M}_{2r-2-2j}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})+\mathcal{N}_{2r-2-2j}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0}))\text{ on }\Gamma,

(A.7)

where
\mathcal{M}_{-1}(w_{0},b_{0}):=|b_{0}|^{2}\text{div }w_{0}-(b_{0}\cdot\nabla)w_{0}\cdot b_{0}
and
\mathcal{N}_{-1}:=0
.

A.2
Regularization of the given data and recovery of compatibility conditions

To construct a smooth data satisfying the compatibility conditions up to infinite order, the first step is to regularize the given data such that we get smooth functions. By the standard approximation of Sobolev function, we know for any given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
(w_{0}^{\varepsilon},b_{0}^{\varepsilon},P_{0}^{\varepsilon})\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that

\|w_{0}^{\varepsilon}-w_{0},b_{0}^{\varepsilon}-b_{0},P_{0}^{\varepsilon}-P_{0}\|_{s}<\varepsilon.

However, such smooth approximation does not preserve the boundary conditions, even for the vanishing boundary conditions for
P_{0}
and
b_{0}^{3}
. So we need to recover the compatibility conditions up to the same order as the given data.

From now on, we assume

•

m=8
, that is, the given data satisfies the compatibility conditions (
A.6
)-(
A.7
) up to 7-th order. This corresponds to the minimal requirement in Theorem
1.3
.

•

\|b_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega})}<\delta_{0}<1
where
\delta_{0}
is a suitably small number to be determined: to absorb the terms containing
(k+2)
-th order derivative arising in
\mathcal{M}_{k}
. Note that we do not need
\|b_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega})}
to be arbitrarily small in the proof.

A.2.1
Recovering the initial constraints

The new data should also satisfy the initial constraints: divergence-free condition of magnetic field, vanishing normal component of magnetic field on the boundary and the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition. The Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition still holds for
P_{0}^{\varepsilon}
, as
-\partial_{3}P_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is just a small perturbation of
-\partial_{3}P_{0}
. We then modify
b_{0}^{\varepsilon}
. First, we introduce
\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}
defined by

\displaystyle\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon,1}=b_{0}^{\varepsilon,1},~~\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon,2}=b_{0}^{\varepsilon,2};~~-\Delta\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon,3}=-\Delta b_{0}^{\varepsilon,3}\text{ in }\Omega,~\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon,3}=0\text{ on }\Gamma,

(A.8)

and then
\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\Omega)
and the elliptic estimates imply
\|\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}-b_{0}\|_{s}\leq\|b_{0}^{\varepsilon}-b_{0}\|_{s}+|0-0|_{s-0.5}=O(\varepsilon)
. Next, we recover the divergence-free condition by introducing
\textbf{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}:=\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}+\nabla\varphi
with
\varphi
determined by

\displaystyle-\Delta\varphi=\text{div }\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}\text{ in }\Omega,~\partial_{3}\varphi=0\text{ on }\Gamma.

(A.9)

With this modification, we now have
\text{div }\textbf{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}=\text{div }\tilde{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}+\Delta\varphi=0
in
\Omega
, and
\textbf{b}_{0}^{3}|_{\Gamma}=0
still holds thanks to the Neumann boundary condition
\partial_{3}\varphi=0\text{ on }\Gamma
. So,
\textbf{b}_{0}^{\varepsilon}
is the desired magnetic field that we need, and it still satisfies a smallness assumption
\|\textbf{b}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\bar{\Omega})}<2\delta_{0}
. We’ll drop
\varepsilon
in
\textbf{b}_{0}
for the sake of clean notations.

A.2.2
Recovering the compatibility conditions up to
(m-1)
-th order

Next we focus on the modification of
w_{0}^{\varepsilon},P_{0}^{\varepsilon}
. After the regularization, we don’t even know if
P_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0
on
\Gamma
holds or not. So the first step is to recover the 0-th order compatibility condition
P_{0}|_{\Gamma}=0
. We define
\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}
by

\displaystyle-\Delta\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}=-\Delta P_{0}^{\varepsilon}\text{ in }\Omega,~~\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}=0\text{ on }\Gamma.

(A.10)

Since
P_{0}|_{\Gamma}=0
, we know

\|\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}-P_{0}\|_{s}\leq\|P_{0}^{\varepsilon}-P_{0}\|_{s}+|0-0|_{s-0.5}=O(\varepsilon).

Next we define
\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)}
to be the following function such that
(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)})
satisfies the compatibility condition up to first order:
\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),1}=w_{0}^{\varepsilon,1},~\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),2}=w_{0}^{\varepsilon,2}
and
\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),3}
is determined by the bi-harmonic system

\begin{cases}\Delta^{2}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),3}=\Delta^{2}w_{0}^{\varepsilon,3}~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),3}=w_{0}^{\varepsilon,3},~~\partial_{3}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),3}=-\overline{\partial}{}_{1}w_{0}^{\varepsilon,1}-\overline{\partial}{}_{2}w_{0}^{\varepsilon,2}+\mathcal{M}_{-1}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0})~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\end{cases}

(A.11)

where
\mathcal{M}_{-1}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0}^{(1)})
is given by (
A.1
). Note that the second boundary contidition only involves
\partial_{3}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)}
because the tangential components are the same of
w_{0}^{\varepsilon}
. So the elliptic estimates give us

\displaystyle\|\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)}-w_{0}\|_{s}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{2}w_{0}^{\varepsilon}-\Delta^{2}w_{0}\|_{s-4}+|w_{0}^{\varepsilon}-w_{0}|_{s-0.5}+|\partial_{3}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)}-\partial_{3}w_{0}|_{s-1.5}

(A.12)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon)+|b_{0}|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}|\nabla\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)}-\nabla w_{0}|_{s-1.5},

where the last term can be absorbed by the left side if we pick
\delta_{0}
sufficiently small. Therefore, by the second boundary condition, we know
(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)})
satisfies the compatibility condition up to first order.

Again, we construct
\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}
such that
(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)})
satisfies the compatibility condition (
A.7
) up to 2nd order. The new pressure is defined by the poly-harmonic system

\begin{cases}-\Delta^{3}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}=-\Delta^{3}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}=\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}=0,~~\partial_{3}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}=\partial_{3}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\\
-\Delta\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}=\mathcal{M}_{0}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0},\mathcal{P}^{(1)})+\mathcal{N}_{0}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0})~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\end{cases}

(A.13)

and thus

\displaystyle\|\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}-P_{0}\|_{s}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{3}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}-\Delta^{3}P_{0}\|_{s-6}+|\partial_{3}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}-\partial_{3}P_{0}|_{s-1.5}

(A.14)

\displaystyle+|\mathcal{M}_{0}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0},\|\mathbf{Q}_{(m+7)}^{(m-1)}\|_{0}+\|\mathbf{Q}_{(m+6)}^{(m-1)}\|_{1}^{(1)})+\mathcal{M}_{0}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0})-\mathcal{M}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})-\mathcal{N}_{0}(w_{0},b_{0})|_{s-2.5}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon)+|b_{0}|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}|\partial_{3}^{2}(\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}-P_{0})|_{s-2.5},

where the last term is again absorbed by the left side if we choose
|b_{0}|\leq\delta_{0}
to be suitably small. It should also be noted that,
\mathcal{M}_{k}
also has other terms containing
(k+2)
-th order derivative, but there are at least two derivatives appearing as
(\bar{b}_{0}\cdot\overline{\nabla})
, and thus we can replace
\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)}
with
\textbf{P}_{0}^{(1)}
using the remaining boundary conditions.

Next we construct
\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2)}
via the following system such that
(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)})
satisfies the compatibility condition (
A.6
) up to 3rd order.

\begin{cases}\Delta^{4}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2),3}=\Delta^{4}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),3}~~&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\partial_{3}^{j}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2),3}=\partial_{3}^{j}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1),3},(0\leq j\leq 2)~~&\text{ on }\Gamma\\
\Delta\text{div }\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2)}=\mathcal{M}_{1}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)})+\mathcal{N}_{1}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)})+\Delta\mathcal{M}_{-1}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0})~~&\text{ on }\Gamma,\end{cases}

(A.15)

and similarly as above we can get

\displaystyle\|\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2)}-w_{0}\|_{s}\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{4}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)}-\Delta^{4}w_{0}\|_{s-8}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}|\partial_{3}^{j}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)}-\partial_{3}^{j}w_{0}|_{s-j-0.5}

(A.16)

\displaystyle+|(\mathcal{M}_{1}+\mathcal{N}_{1}+\Delta\mathcal{M}_{-1})(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(1)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(2)})-(\mathcal{M}_{1}+\mathcal{N}_{1}+\Delta\mathcal{M}_{-1})(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})|_{s-3.5}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle O(\varepsilon)+|b_{0}|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}|\partial_{3}^{3}(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(2)}-w_{0})|_{s-3.5},

where the last term is again absorbed by the left side if we choose
|b_{0}|\leq\delta_{0}
to be suitably small.

So, we can repeat the above procedures such that
\textbf{P}_{0}^{(m)}
is determined by the poly-harmonic equation
\Delta^{2m-1}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(m)}=\Delta^{2m-1}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(m-1)}
in
\Omega
equipped with the boundary conditions
\partial_{3}^{j}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(m)}=\partial_{3}^{j}\textbf{P}_{0}^{(m-1)}
on
\Gamma
for
0\leq j\leq 2m-3
and the compatibility condition (
A.7
) for the case
k=2m-2
with
(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
replaced by
(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m-1)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(m)})
.

Similarly,
\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m)}
is determined by
\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m),1,2}=\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m-1),1,2}
and
\Delta^{2m}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m),3}=\Delta^{2m}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m-1),3}
in
\Omega
equipped with the boundary conditions
\partial_{3}^{j}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m),3}=\partial_{3}^{j}\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m-1),3}
on
\Gamma
for
0\leq j\leq 2m-2
and the compatibility condition (
A.6
) for the case
k=2m-1
with
(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
replaced by
(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(m)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(m)})
.

Since the given rough data
(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
satisfies the compatibility conditions up to 7-th order, we stop the above procedure after we get
(\textbf{w}_{0}^{(4)},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{P}_{0}^{(4)})
which is a smooth data and also satisfies the compatibility conditions up to 7-th order. We rename this smooth data to be
(\textbf{v}_{0},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{Q}_{0})
. For any given
\varepsilon>0
, we construct a smooth data
(\textbf{v}_{0},\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{Q}_{0})
that satisfies the compatibility conditions up to the same order as the given rough data
(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
and has the following approximation

\|\textbf{v}_{0}-w_{0}\|_{8}+\|\textbf{b}_{0}-b_{0}\|_{8}+\|\textbf{Q}_{0}-P_{0}\|_{8}=O(\varepsilon).

(A.17)

A.3
Extend the compatibility conditions up to infinite order

A.3.1
Formal constructions

We then try to extend the initial data such that the compatibility conditions are fulfilled up to infinite order. First we briefly state some formal construction. Recall in section
A
, for a given data
(w_{0},b_{0},P_{0})
, the corresonding solution satisfies the wave equation

\partial_{t}^{2}P-\Delta_{b}P=\mathcal{M}_{0}(v,b,P)+\mathcal{N}_{0}(v,b),~~\Delta_{b}:=(1+|b|^{2})\Delta-(b\cdot\partial)^{2},

and
\mathcal{M}_{0}(v,b,P):=(b_{0}\cdot\partial)^{2}b\cdot b+\mathcal{R}(v,b)
where
\mathcal{R}
only contains the first-order derivatives of
b,v
. So if we start with an irrotational velocity
w_{0}=\nabla\psi
and define
P_{(-1)}:=-\psi
, then since
P=p+\frac{1}{2}|b|^{2}
we have

\partial_{t}P=\partial_{t}p+b\cdot\partial_{t}b=-\text{div }w+b\cdot((b\cdot\partial)w-b\text{div }w)=-(1+|b|^{2})\text{div }w+(b\cdot\partial)w\cdot b,

which then gives, after restricting it to
\{t=0\}

-\Delta_{b}P_{(-1)}=-P_{(1)}+(b_{0}\cdot\partial)\nabla\psi\cdot b_{0},

where the right side only depends on the given data of velocity and magnetic field. Taking more time derivatives and setting
t=0
yields an infinite elliptic system of the form

-\Delta_{\textbf{b}_{0}}P_{(k)}=-P_{(k+2)}+\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\prime}(P_{(-1)},\cdots,P_{(k-1)}),~~k\geq-1,

where
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\prime}
is a functional that only depends on the derivatives of its arguments and
b_{0}
up to a certain order. This system has similar structure as in
[
40
, Lemma 16.1]
and thus can be solved in a similar manner. The only difference comes from the appearance of magnetic field, but
(b_{0}\cdot\partial)
is a tangential derivatiev and we can pick suitable
b_{0}
such that its normal component vanishes in a neighborhood of the boundary.

A.3.2
Full construction procedure

For specific calculations, we now define the desired smooth data
(\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty},\textbf{b}_{0}^{\infty},\textbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty})
by

\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty}:=\textbf{v}_{0}-\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{(-1)}^{\infty},~~\textbf{b}_{0}^{\infty}:=\textbf{b}_{0}.

(A.18)

And after a long and tedious calculation, we find
\textbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty}
is determined by the following infinite elliptic system in
\Omega
, where
\lambda:=1+|\textbf{b}_{0}|^{2}
.

\displaystyle-\lambda\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{-1}^{\infty}=

\displaystyle-(\mathbf{Q}_{(1)}^{\infty}-\mathbf{Q}_{(1)})-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{Q}_{-1}^{\infty}+\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{-1}(\textbf{b}_{0},\mathbf{Q}_{-1}^{\infty})

(A.19)

\displaystyle-\lambda\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty}=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{Q}_{(2)}^{\infty}-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty}+(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0}+\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{0}(\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{-1}^{\infty})

(A.20)

\displaystyle-\lambda\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{(1)}^{\infty}=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{Q}_{(3)}^{\infty}-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{\infty}+(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{3}\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0}-|\textbf{b}_{0}|^{2}(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}(\nabla\cdot\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty})+\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{1}(\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{-1}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty}),

(A.21)

\displaystyle-\lambda\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{(2)}^{\infty}=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{Q}_{(4)}^{\infty}-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty}+\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{2}(\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{-1}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{(1)}^{\infty})

(A.22)

and for
k\geq 2

\displaystyle-\lambda\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{(k)}^{\infty}=

\displaystyle-\mathbf{Q}_{(k+2)}^{\infty}-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{(k-2)}^{\infty}+\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{k}(\textbf{b}_{0},\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{-1}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{(1)}^{\infty},\cdots,\mathbf{Q}_{(k-1)}^{\infty}),

(A.23)

with vanishing boundary conditions for each
\mathbf{Q}_{(k)}
. These
\mathcal{N}_{k}^{\prime}
’s have the following structure

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{k}(\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty},\textbf{b}_{0},\mathbf{Q}^{\infty}_{(-1)},\cdots,\mathbf{Q}^{(\infty)}_{(k-1)})

(A.24)

\displaystyle=P(\textbf{b}_{0})C^{k;m_{1}\cdots m_{r}}_{i_{1}\cdots i_{m},j_{1}\cdots j_{n},k_{1}\cdots k_{r}}\left((\nabla^{i_{1}}\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty})\cdots(\nabla^{i_{m}}\textbf{v}_{0}^{\infty})(\nabla^{j_{1}}\textbf{b}_{0})\cdots(\nabla^{j_{n}}\textbf{b}_{0})(\nabla^{k_{1}}\mathbf{Q}_{(m_{1})})\cdots(\nabla^{k_{r}}\mathbf{Q}_{(m_{l})})\right),

where the indices satisfy

\displaystyle i_{1}+\cdots+i_{m}+j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n}+(k_{1}+m_{1})+\cdots+(k_{r}+m_{r})=k+2,

\displaystyle 1\leq i_{1},\cdots,i_{m},j_{1},\cdots,j_{m},k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}\leq k+1,

\displaystyle-1\leq m_{1},\cdots,m_{r}\leq k-1,~1\leq k_{1}+m_{1},\cdots,k_{r}+m_{r}\leq k+1.

Remark
.

Note that (
A.22
) is derived from taking two time derivatives in (
A.20
). On can also In fact, taking two time derivatives in (
A.20
), we know the right side has top-order terms
(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{Q}_{2}-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0}
. For the latter term
(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0}
, we recall that
\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{q}+\frac{1}{2}|\textbf{b}_{0}|^{2}
. Taking one time derivative and using continuity equation, we get
\mathbf{Q}_{(1)}=-\text{div }\textbf{v}_{0}+\mathbf{b}_{1}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0}
. Taking one more time derivative and using the momentum equation
\mathbf{v}_{1}=(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\textbf{b}_{0}-\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{0}
, we get
\text{div }\mathbf{v}_{1}=\text{div }(-\nabla\mathbf{Q}_{0}+(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\textbf{b}_{0})
. Using
\text{div }\textbf{b}_{0}=0
we know
\text{div }(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\textbf{b}_{0}
is of lower order. So we have
\mathbf{Q}_{(2)}\sim\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{0}+\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0}
, and thus

(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{Q}_{2}-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\mathbf{b}_{2}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0}=(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{0}+\text{ lower order terms.}

We choose to write in this form because it makes equations shorter. Alternatively one can differentiate (
A.21
) in time variable again and again to get the form
-\Delta_{\textbf{b}_{0}}\mathbf{Q}_{(k)}^{\infty}=-\mathbf{Q}_{(k+2)}^{\infty}+\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\prime}(\textbf{b}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{(-1)},\cdots,\mathbf{Q}_{(k-1)})+\mathcal{N}^{\prime}_{k}(\textbf{b}_{0}^{\infty},\mathbf{Q}_{(-1)},\cdots,\mathbf{Q}_{(k-1)})
where
\Delta_{\textbf{b}_{0}}:=(1+|\textbf{b}_{0}|^{2})\Delta-(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)^{2}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{k}^{\prime}
denotes the terms containing
(k+2)
-th order derivative.

This elliptic system has a parallel structure as
[
40
, (16.11)]
. Following
[
40
, Lemma 16.2]
, we impose the system with boundary conditions

\mathbf{Q}_{(k)}^{\infty}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{Q}_{0,k},~~-\nabla_{N}\mathbf{Q}_{(k)}^{\infty}|_{\Gamma}=\mathbf{Q}_{1,k},~~k\geq-1.

Then the system has a formal power series in the distance to the boundary

\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{(k)}(r,\omega)\sim\sum\mathbf{Q}_{n,k}(\omega)\frac{(1-r)^{n}}{n!},

where
r
is the distance to the boundary and
\omega
is the angular variable. Let
0\leq\chi(\cdot)\leq 1
be a smooth bump function on
\mathbb{R}
that equals to 1 in
[-1,1]
and vanishes outside
[-2,2]
. Then, by
[
40
, Lemma 16.2]
, there exist
\varepsilon_{k,n}
such that

\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{(k)}(r,\omega)=\sum\chi\left(\frac{1-r}{\varepsilon_{k,n}}\right)\mathbf{Q}_{n,k}(\omega)\frac{(1-r)^{n}}{n!},

such that the above elliptic system holds to infinite order on the boundary. Note that
(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)
is tangential on the boundary and
\textbf{b}_{0}
has smallness assumption, so the extra terms involving
\textbf{b}_{0}
will not affect the convergence of the power series.

Now let
(\tilde{\textbf{v}_{0}},\textbf{b}_{0},\tilde{\textbf{Q}_{0}})
are functions that vanish to infinite order on the boundary. Define

\textbf{w}_{0}^{\infty}:=\tilde{\textbf{v}_{0}}-\nabla\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{(-1)},~\textbf{P}_{0}^{\infty}:=\tilde{\textbf{Q}_{0}}+\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{0},~\mathbf{P}_{(1)}:=-(1+|\textbf{b}_{0}|^{2})(\text{div }\tilde{\textbf{v}_{0}}+\Delta\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{(-1)})+(\textbf{b}_{0}\cdot\nabla)\textbf{w}_{0}^{\infty}\cdot\textbf{b}_{0},

where
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{0},\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{(-1)}
are given by the above construction. Then inductively one can show that
\mathbf{P}_{(k)}^{\infty}=\tilde{\textbf{Q}_{0}}_{(k)}+\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{(k)}
, where
\overline{\mathbf{Q}}_{(k)}
are constructed above and
\tilde{\textbf{Q}_{0}}_{(k)}
vanishes to infinite order on the boundary. Hence, choosing boundary data such that
\mathbf{Q}_{0,k}=0
for
k\geq 0
and
\mathbf{Q}_{1,k}\geq c_{0}>0
, then the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition for
\textbf{P}_{0}^{\infty}
is fulfilled and also we have
\mathbf{P}^{\infty}_{(k)}|_{\Gamma}=0
for all
k\geq 0
.
